I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. It's more about this... feeling of losing control. If I step on the scale and it's up even a pound—like maybe after we have a big station meal or I drink extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm out of control. It's just a really overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started cutting down your meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My gear and my station clothes definitely hang a lot looser on me now. We had a fitness review at the department recently, and they actually flagged my weight... they told me it was too low for my build. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I've been cutting my meals down a lot. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where a normal day is just me having black coffee, a protein shake, and a really small dinner. And I'm doing that even on shift days at the station, when I know for a fact I've burned way more than what I'm taking in.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. Honestly, if the scale is up even just a pound—like maybe after a big meal at the station or just from drinking extra water—I get this... this tight panic. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just start thinking that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Other people at the station probably just see a guy in shape, but... I don't really see it that way. When I look in the mirror before work, I really focus in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. And if they don't, it just messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. It's like my whole mindset depends on what I see in that mirror, even if no one else notices it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty strict about what I eat, so I don't really ever let myself go overboard like that. I don't really have moments where I lose control and eat a ton of food all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I just... I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with eating so little to not gain any weight at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess I came in because... what started out as just trying to eat cleaner to get on the lieutenant track has kind of turned into something else. I've been cutting my meals down a lot. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where a normal day is just me having black coffee, a protein shake, and a really small dinner. And I'm doing that even on shift days at the station, when I know for a fact I've burned way more than what I'm taking in.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. Honestly, if the scale is up even just a pound—like maybe after a big meal at the station or just from drinking extra water—I get this... this tight panic. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just start thinking that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Other people at the station probably just see a guy in shape, but... I don't really see it that way. When I look in the mirror before work, I really focus in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. And if they don't, it just messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. It's like my whole mindset depends on what I see in that mirror, even if no one else notices it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty strict about what I eat, so I don't really ever let myself go overboard like that. Even on days when I'm really hungry after a tough call or a long shift, I just stick to my routine. I don't really have moments where I lose control and eat a ton of food all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I just... I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. It's more about this... feeling of losing control. If I step on the scale and it's up even a pound—like maybe after we have a big station meal or I drink extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm out of control. It's just a really overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started cutting down your meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My gear and my station clothes definitely hang a lot looser on me now. We had a fitness review at the department recently, and they actually flagged my weight... they told me it was too low for my build. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with eating so little to not gain any weight at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. If anything, I'm just trying to make sure I don't put on any weight, and keeping my meals small is the only way I feel like I can guarantee that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: I think it's a mix of both. Keeping the weight off is a big deal, like I mentioned, but the shape definitely matters too. Like... when I'm getting ready for a shift and I look in the mirror, I'm always zeroing in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. If they don't, if I look even a little soft, it really messes with me. It just throws off how confident and, I guess, command-ready I feel for the rest of the day at the station. So yeah, achieving that specific look is definitely a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how you see yourself as a person, beyond just how you look or perform as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, I haven't really thought about it like that. It really is mostly just about the job and wanting to look the part. I don't feel like my weight defines who I am as a whole person, or my personality, or anything like that. It's just... I just want to stay sharp for work and feel good about how I look, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to have a sharp waist and face more about achieving a certain appearance or about feeling fit for your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but mostly it's about the appearance. I mean, being fit for the job is important, obviously, because it's physical work. But when I'm checking the mirror, I'm not really thinking about if I can carry a hose or climb a ladder. I'm just looking at the angles... making sure I don't look soft. So yeah, I'd say it's more about achieving that specific look.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess I came in because... what started out as just trying to eat cleaner to get on the lieutenant track has kind of turned into something else. I've been cutting my meals down a lot. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where a normal day is just me having black coffee, a protein shake, and a really small dinner. And I'm doing that even on shift days at the station, when I know for a fact I've burned way more than what I'm taking in.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. Honestly, if the scale is up even just a pound—like maybe after a big meal at the station or just from drinking extra water—I get this... this tight panic. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just start thinking that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Other people at the station probably just see a guy in shape, but... I don't really see it that way. When I look in the mirror before work, I really focus in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. And if they don't, it just messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. It's like my whole mindset depends on what I see in that mirror, even if no one else notices it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty strict about what I eat, so I don't really ever let myself go overboard like that. Even on days when I'm really hungry after a tough call or a long shift, I just stick to my routine. I don't really have moments where I lose control and eat a ton of food all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I just... I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. It's more about this... feeling of losing control. If I step on the scale and it's up even a pound—like maybe after we have a big station meal or I drink extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm out of control. It's just a really overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started cutting down your meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My gear and my station clothes definitely hang a lot looser on me now. We had a fitness review at the department recently, and they actually flagged my weight... they told me it was too low for my build. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with eating so little to not gain any weight at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. If anything, I'm just trying to make sure I don't put on any weight, and keeping my meals small is the only way I feel like I can guarantee that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: I think it's a mix of both. Keeping the weight off is a big deal, like I mentioned, but the shape definitely matters too. Like... when I'm getting ready for a shift and I look in the mirror, I'm always zeroing in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. If they don't, if I look even a little soft, it really messes with me. It just throws off how confident and, I guess, command-ready I feel for the rest of the day at the station. So yeah, achieving that specific look is definitely a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how you see yourself as a person, beyond just how you look or perform as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, I haven't really thought about it like that. It really is mostly just about the job and wanting to look the part. I don't feel like my weight defines who I am as a whole person, or my personality, or anything like that. It's just... I just want to stay sharp for work and feel good about how I look, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to have a sharp waist and face more about achieving a certain appearance or about feeling fit for your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but mostly it's about the appearance. I mean, being fit for the job is important, obviously, because it's physical work. But when I'm checking the mirror, I'm not really thinking about if I can carry a hose or climb a ladder. I'm just looking at the angles... making sure I don't look soft. So yeah, I'd say it's more about achieving that specific look.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I feel like we kind of just went over this, but... yeah, it's a mix of both. Like I said a minute ago, avoiding weight gain is definitely a big part of it, but getting that specific shape—just making sure my face and waist look sharp and not soft—is really what I'm focused on the most.\n\nDoctor: Which is more important to you, not gaining weight or achieving your desired body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know how to separate the two. Like I was just saying, they kind of go hand in hand for me. If I gain weight, I lose the shape I want. So... I guess I don't really have a clear answer on which one is strictly more important. They just both matter to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess I came in because... what started out as just trying to eat cleaner to get on the lieutenant track has kind of turned into something else. I've been cutting my meals down a lot. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where a normal day is just me having black coffee, a protein shake, and a really small dinner. And I'm doing that even on shift days at the station, when I know for a fact I've burned way more than what I'm taking in.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. Honestly, if the scale is up even just a pound—like maybe after a big meal at the station or just from drinking extra water—I get this... this tight panic. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just start thinking that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Other people at the station probably just see a guy in shape, but... I don't really see it that way. When I look in the mirror before work, I really focus in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. And if they don't, it just messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. It's like my whole mindset depends on what I see in that mirror, even if no one else notices it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty strict about what I eat, so I don't really ever let myself go overboard like that. Even on days when I'm really hungry after a tough call or a long shift, I just stick to my routine. I don't really have moments where I lose control and eat a ton of food all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I just... I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. It's more about this... feeling of losing control. If I step on the scale and it's up even a pound—like maybe after we have a big station meal or I drink extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm out of control. It's just a really overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started cutting down your meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My gear and my station clothes definitely hang a lot looser on me now. We had a fitness review at the department recently, and they actually flagged my weight... they told me it was too low for my build. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with eating so little to not gain any weight at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. If anything, I'm just trying to make sure I don't put on any weight, and keeping my meals small is the only way I feel like I can guarantee that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: I think it's a mix of both. Keeping the weight off is a big deal, like I mentioned, but the shape definitely matters too. Like... when I'm getting ready for a shift and I look in the mirror, I'm always zeroing in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. If they don't, if I look even a little soft, it really messes with me. It just throws off how confident and, I guess, command-ready I feel for the rest of the day at the station. So yeah, achieving that specific look is definitely a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how you see yourself as a person, beyond just how you look or perform as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, I haven't really thought about it like that. It really is mostly just about the job and wanting to look the part. I don't feel like my weight defines who I am as a whole person, or my personality, or anything like that. It's just... I just want to stay sharp for work and feel good about how I look, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to have a sharp waist and face more about achieving a certain appearance or about feeling fit for your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but mostly it's about the appearance. I mean, being fit for the job is important, obviously, because it's physical work. But when I'm checking the mirror, I'm not really thinking about if I can carry a hose or climb a ladder. I'm just looking at the angles... making sure I don't look soft. So yeah, I'd say it's more about achieving that specific look.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I feel like we kind of just went over this, but... yeah, it's a mix of both. Like I said a minute ago, avoiding weight gain is definitely a big part of it, but getting that specific shape—just making sure my face and waist look sharp and not soft—is really what I'm focused on the most.\n\nDoctor: Which is more important to you, not gaining weight or achieving your desired body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know how to separate the two. Like I was just saying, they kind of go hand in hand for me. If I gain weight, I lose the shape I want. So... I guess I don't really have a clear answer on which one is strictly more important. They just both matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it possible for you to imagine being at a healthy weight but not having the sharp face and waist you desire, and if so, would that be acceptable to you?\n\nPatient: I don't think that would be acceptable to me, no. If I was at a... quote-unquote healthy weight, but I lost that sharpness, it would really bother me. When I look in the mirror before my shift, I'm specifically checking to see if my waist and face look sharp enough. If they don't... it just messes with my head. It changes how confident I feel going into work, like I'm not as command-ready for the day. So just being a certain weight wouldn't be enough if the shape wasn't right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Other people at the station probably just see a guy in shape, but... I don't really see it that way. When I look in the mirror before work, I really focus in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. And if they don't, it just messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. It's like my whole mindset depends on what I see in that mirror, even if no one else notices it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty strict about what I eat, so I don't really ever let myself go overboard like that. Even on days when I'm really hungry after a tough call or a long shift, I just stick to my routine. I don't really have moments where I lose control and eat a ton of food all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I just... I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. It's more about this... feeling of losing control. If I step on the scale and it's up even a pound—like maybe after we have a big station meal or I drink extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm out of control. It's just a really overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started cutting down your meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My gear and my station clothes definitely hang a lot looser on me now. We had a fitness review at the department recently, and they actually flagged my weight... they told me it was too low for my build. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with eating so little to not gain any weight at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. If anything, I'm just trying to make sure I don't put on any weight, and keeping my meals small is the only way I feel like I can guarantee that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: I think it's a mix of both. Keeping the weight off is a big deal, like I mentioned, but the shape definitely matters too. Like... when I'm getting ready for a shift and I look in the mirror, I'm always zeroing in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. If they don't, if I look even a little soft, it really messes with me. It just throws off how confident and, I guess, command-ready I feel for the rest of the day at the station. So yeah, achieving that specific look is definitely a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how you see yourself as a person, beyond just how you look or perform as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, being fit for the job is important, obviously, because it's physical work. But when I'm checking the mirror, I'm not really thinking about if I can carry a hose or climb a ladder. I'm just looking at the angles... making sure I don't look soft. So yeah, I'd say it's more about achieving that specific look.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I feel like we kind of just went over this, but... yeah, it's a mix of both. Like I said a minute ago, avoiding weight gain is definitely a big part of it, but getting that specific shape—just making sure my face and waist look sharp and not soft—is really what I'm focused on the most.\n\nDoctor: Which is more important to you, not gaining weight or achieving your desired body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know how to separate the two. So I think even if I had the exact shape I wanted, that fear of the number going up wouldn't just disappear.

I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess I came in because... what started out as just trying to eat cleaner to get on the lieutenant track has kind of turned into something else. I've been cutting my meals down a lot. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where a normal day is just me having black coffee, a protein shake, and a really small dinner. And I'm doing that even on shift days at the station, when I know for a fact I've burned way more than what I'm taking in.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. Honestly, if the scale is up even just a pound—like maybe after a big meal at the station or just from drinking extra water—I get this... this tight panic. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just start thinking that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Other people at the station probably just see a guy in shape, but... I don't really see it that way. When I look in the mirror before work, I really focus in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. And if they don't, it just messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. It's like my whole mindset depends on what I see in that mirror, even if no one else notices it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty strict about what I eat, so I don't really ever let myself go overboard like that. Even on days when I'm really hungry after a tough call or a long shift, I just stick to my routine. I don't really have moments where I lose control and eat a ton of food all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I just... I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. It's more about this... feeling of losing control. If I step on the scale and it's up even a pound—like maybe after we have a big station meal or I drink extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm out of control. It's just a really overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started cutting down your meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My gear and my station clothes definitely hang a lot looser on me now. We had a fitness review at the department recently, and they actually flagged my weight... they told me it was too low for my build. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with eating so little to not gain any weight at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. If anything, I'm just trying to make sure I don't put on any weight, and keeping my meals small is the only way I feel like I can guarantee that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: I think it's a mix of both. Keeping the weight off is a big deal, like I mentioned, but the shape definitely matters too. Like... when I'm getting ready for a shift and I look in the mirror, I'm always zeroing in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. If they don't, if I look even a little soft, it really messes with me. It just throws off how confident and, I guess, command-ready I feel for the rest of the day at the station. So yeah, achieving that specific look is definitely a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how you see yourself as a person, beyond just how you look or perform as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, I haven't really thought about it like that. It really is mostly just about the job and wanting to look the part. I don't feel like my weight defines who I am as a whole person, or my personality, or anything like that. It's just... I just want to stay sharp for work and feel good about how I look, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to have a sharp waist and face more about achieving a certain appearance or about feeling fit for your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but mostly it's about the appearance. I mean, being fit for the job is important, obviously, because it's physical work. But when I'm checking the mirror, I'm not really thinking about if I can carry a hose or climb a ladder. I'm just looking at the angles... making sure I don't look soft. So yeah, I'd say it's more about achieving that specific look.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I feel like we kind of just went over this, but... yeah, it's a mix of both. Like I said a minute ago, avoiding weight gain is definitely a big part of it, but getting that specific shape—just making sure my face and waist look sharp and not soft—is really what I'm focused on the most.\n\nDoctor: Which is more important to you, not gaining weight or achieving your desired body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know how to separate the two. Like I was just saying, they kind of go hand in hand for me. If I gain weight, I lose the shape I want. So... I guess I don't really have a clear answer on which one is strictly more important. They just both matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it possible for you to imagine being at a healthy weight but not having the sharp face and waist you desire, and if so, would that be acceptable to you?\n\nPatient: I don't think that would be acceptable to me, no. If I was at a... quote-unquote healthy weight, but I lost that sharpness, it would really bother me. When I look in the mirror before my shift, I'm specifically checking to see if my waist and face look sharp enough. If they don't... it just messes with my head. It changes how confident I feel going into work, like I'm not as command-ready for the day. So just being a certain weight wouldn't be enough if the shape wasn't right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would continue to restrict your food intake if you achieved your desired body shape but were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I've never actually been in that situation, so... it's hard to say for sure. I guess if I had the exact shape I wanted, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to restrict as much? But... I'm just not sure if I'd trust it to stay that way if I started eating more. I really don't know.\n\nDoctor: If you were to achieve your desired sharp face and waist, would you still be afraid of gaining even a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'm pretty sure I would still be afraid. Honestly, even just a small amount... if the scale is up by even a single pound, like after a big station meal with the guys or just from drinking extra water, I get this tight panic. It just feels like I’ve completely blown it. I immediately start worrying that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm out of control. So I think even if I had the exact shape I wanted, that fear of the number going up wouldn't just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restrictive eating is more about avoiding the feeling of being overweight or more about achieving a specific physical appearance?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's definitely more about achieving that specific appearance. Like I mentioned a minute ago, it really comes down to what I see in the mirror before work. I'm focused on making sure my waist and face look sharp enough. If they don't have that look, it just completely messes with my confidence. I just don't feel command-ready for the day. So, yeah, getting that exact physical shape is really what drives it for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess I came in because... what started out as just trying to eat cleaner to get on the lieutenant track has kind of turned into something else. I've been cutting my meals down a lot. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where a normal day is just me having black coffee, a protein shake, and a really small dinner. And I'm doing that even on shift days at the station, when I know for a fact I've burned way more than what I'm taking in.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. Honestly, if the scale is up even just a pound—like maybe after a big meal at the station or just from drinking extra water—I get this... this tight panic. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just start thinking that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Other people at the station probably just see a guy in shape, but... I don't really see it that way. When I look in the mirror before work, I really focus in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. And if they don't, it just messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. It's like my whole mindset depends on what I see in that mirror, even if no one else notices it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty strict about what I eat, so I don't really ever let myself go overboard like that. Even on days when I'm really hungry after a tough call or a long shift, I just stick to my routine. I don't really have moments where I lose control and eat a ton of food all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I just... I control what goes in, so I don't ever feel the need to do something like that to get rid of it. It's just not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I work out regularly, but that's mostly because I have to stay fit for the job. Being at the firehouse, you need to be physically ready for whatever happens. But I don't... I don't go to the gym just to burn off calories or spend hours on the treadmill because of something I ate. I just stick to my normal training routine and leave it at that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it would affect your appearance as a firefighter, or is it also about something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but it's not just about looking the part for the job. It's more about this... feeling of losing control. If I step on the scale and it's up even a pound—like maybe after we have a big station meal or I drink extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm out of control. It's just a really overwhelming fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started cutting down your meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My gear and my station clothes definitely hang a lot looser on me now. We had a fitness review at the department recently, and they actually flagged my weight... they told me it was too low for my build. But honestly, even after hearing that, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little bit more.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with eating so little to not gain any weight at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. If anything, I'm just trying to make sure I don't put on any weight, and keeping my meals small is the only way I feel like I can guarantee that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: I think it's a mix of both. Keeping the weight off is a big deal, like I mentioned, but the shape definitely matters too. Like... when I'm getting ready for a shift and I look in the mirror, I'm always zeroing in on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. If they don't, if I look even a little soft, it really messes with me. It just throws off how confident and, I guess, command-ready I feel for the rest of the day at the station. So yeah, achieving that specific look is definitely a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how you see yourself as a person, beyond just how you look or perform as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, I haven't really thought about it like that. It really is mostly just about the job and wanting to look the part. I don't feel like my weight defines who I am as a whole person, or my personality, or anything like that. It's just... I just want to stay sharp for work and feel good about how I look, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to have a sharp waist and face more about achieving a certain appearance or about feeling fit for your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I guess it's a bit of both, but mostly it's about the appearance. I mean, being fit for the job is important, obviously, because it's physical work. But when I'm checking the mirror, I'm not really thinking about if I can carry a hose or climb a ladder. I'm just looking at the angles... making sure I don't look soft. So yeah, I'd say it's more about achieving that specific look.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I feel like we kind of just went over this, but... yeah, it's a mix of both. Like I said a minute ago, avoiding weight gain is definitely a big part of it, but getting that specific shape—just making sure my face and waist look sharp and not soft—is really what I'm focused on the most.\n\nDoctor: Which is more important to you, not gaining weight or achieving your desired body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know how to separate the two. Like I was just saying, they kind of go hand in hand for me. If I gain weight, I lose the shape I want. So... I guess I don't really have a clear answer on which one is strictly more important. They just both matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it possible for you to imagine being at a healthy weight but not having the sharp face and waist you desire, and if so, would that be acceptable to you?\n\nPatient: I don't think that would be acceptable to me, no. If I was at a... quote-unquote healthy weight, but I lost that sharpness, it would really bother me. When I look in the mirror before my shift, I'm specifically checking to see if my waist and face look sharp enough. If they don't... it just messes with my head. It changes how confident I feel going into work, like I'm not as command-ready for the day. So just being a certain weight wouldn't be enough if the shape wasn't right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would continue to restrict your food intake if you achieved your desired body shape but were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I've never actually been in that situation, so... it's hard to say for sure. I guess if I had the exact shape I wanted, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to restrict as much? But... I'm just not sure if I'd trust it to stay that way if I started eating more. I really don't know.\n\nDoctor: If you were to achieve your desired sharp face and waist, would you still be afraid of gaining even a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'm pretty sure I would still be afraid. Honestly, even just a small amount... if the scale is up by even a single pound, like after a big station meal with the guys or just from drinking extra water, I get this tight panic. It just feels like I’ve completely blown it. I immediately start worrying that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm out of control. So I think even if I had the exact shape I wanted, that fear of the number going up wouldn't just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restrictive eating is more about avoiding the feeling of being overweight or more about achieving a specific physical appearance?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's definitely more about achieving that specific appearance. Like I mentioned a minute ago, it really comes down to what I see in the mirror before work. I'm focused on making sure my waist and face look sharp enough. If they don't have that look, it just completely messes with my confidence. I just don't feel command-ready for the day. So, yeah, getting that exact physical shape is really what drives it for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever feel comfortable with your body, even if it didn't have the sharp face and waist you're aiming for?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don't think I could. Like I was just saying a second ago, it really all comes back to that. When I look in the mirror before heading into work, I'm specifically checking to see if my waist and face look sharp enough. If they don't have that look, it just completely messes with my confidence. I need to see that to feel command-ready for the day. So... if I didn't have that sharpness at all, I just wouldn't feel comfortable in my own skin.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's really the fear of what gaining weight means. Like I said earlier, if the scale goes up even a single pound—whether it's from drinking too much water or eating a big meal at the station with the guys—I just get this tight panic. It feels like I've completely blown it. I just worry that people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm losing control. So, yeah, it's definitely that fear driving it.

I mean, the idea of the number on the scale going up at all just... it really stresses me out. I don't want to gain anything back. If anything, I'm just trying to make sure I don't put on any weight, and keeping my meals small is the only way I feel like I can guarantee that.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: I think it's a mix of both. So yeah, achieving that specific look is definitely a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how you see yourself as a person, beyond just how you look or perform as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I'm just looking at the angles... making sure I don't look soft. So yeah, I'd say it's more about achieving that specific look.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a certain body shape?\n\nPatient: I feel like we kind of just went over this, but... yeah, it's a mix of both. Like I said a minute ago, avoiding weight gain is definitely a big part of it, but getting that specific shape—just making sure my face and waist look sharp and not soft—is really what I'm focused on the most.\n\nDoctor: Which is more important to you, not gaining weight or achieving your desired body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know how to separate the two. They just both matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it possible for you to imagine being at a healthy weight but not having the sharp face and waist you desire, and if so, would that be acceptable to you?\n\nPatient: I don't think that would be acceptable to me, no.
